The Euclidean ϕ 2 4 \phi^{4}_{2} theory as a limit of an inhomogeneous Bose gas
Cristina Caraci, Antti Knowles, Alessio Ranallo, Pedro Torres Giesteira

Abstract
We prove that the grand canonical Gibbs state of an interacting two-dimensional quantum Bose gas confined by a trapping potential converges to the complex Euclidean field theory with local quartic self-interaction, when the density of the gas becomes large and the range of the interaction becomes small. We obtain convergence of the relative partition function and convergence in L 1 ∩ L ∞ L^{1}\cap L^{\infty} of the renormalised reduced density matrices. The field theory is supported on distributions of negative regularity, which requires a renormalisation by divergent mass and energy counterterms. Unlike previous results in the homogeneous setting of the torus without a trapping potential, the counterterms are not given by a finite collection of scalars but by diverging counterterm functions. This leads to significant new mathematical challenges. For our proof, we also derive quantitative bounds on the Green function of Schrödinger operators and of its gradient, which might be of independent interest.



1. Introduction

A Euclidean field theory of a scalar complex field on a domain
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is specified by a formal probability measure on a space of fields
1
1

1

Rigorously, the space of fields is the space of complex-valued distributions over
\Lambda
.

\phi\vcentcolon\Lambda\to\mathbb{C}
of the form

\rho(\mathrm{d}\phi)=\frac{1}{c}e^{-S(\phi)}\,\mathrm{D}\phi\,,

(1.1)

where
\mathrm{D}\phi=\prod_{x\in\Lambda}\mathrm{d}\phi(x)
is the formal uniform measure on the space of fields, and
S
is the action.
One of the simplest field theories with nontrivial interaction is the
Euclidean
\phi^{4}_{d}
theory
, whose action is given by

S(\phi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{d}x\,\bar{\phi}(x)\biggl(-\frac{\Delta}{2}-\theta+U(x)\biggr)\phi(x)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{d}x\,|\phi(x)|^{4}\,,

(1.2)

where
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
is a constant,
\lambda>0
a coupling constant,
\Delta
is the Laplacian on
\Lambda
, and
U\vcentcolon\Lambda\to\mathbb{R}
is an external potential.

Euclidean field theories originally arose in high-energy physics in
d=4
space-time dimensions
21
;
17
. Subsequently, Euclidean field theories have proven of great importance in statistical mechanics in
d\leqslant 3
dimensions. The works
24
;
25
recognised the analogy between Euclidean field theories and classical statistical mechanics, which was followed by a purely probabilistic formulation of Euclidean field theories in
20
;
19
. The rigorous study of field theories of the form (
1.1
) has been a major topic in mathematical physics since the late sixties; see e.g.
9
;
22
;
10
for reviews. The basic philosophy in all of these works is to include the quadratic part of (
1.2
) in a free, Gaussian, measure

\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{d}\phi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{c_{0}}\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{d}x\,\bar{\phi}(x)(-\frac{\Delta}{2}-\theta+U(x))\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{D}\phi\,,

which can be easily made sense of. The quartic part of (
1.2
),

V(\phi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{\Lambda}\mathrm{d}x\,|\phi(x)|^{4}\,,

is regarded as an interaction that allows one to write (
1.1
) in the form

\rho(\mathrm{d}\phi)=\frac{1}{\zeta}\,\mathrm{e}^{-V(\phi)}\,\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{d}\phi)\,,\qquad\zeta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{c}{c_{0}}\,.

(1.3)

The fundamental difficulty in such an undertaking arises from ultraviolet divergencies: in dimensions greater than one, the field
\phi
is
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely a distribution of negative regularity, and hence the interaction
V(\phi)
is ill-defined. As a consequence, to make rigorous sense of
\rho
, the interaction has to be renormalised by subtracting suitably chosen infinite counterterms.

In this paper we identify the Euclidean field theory of the form (
1.1
) with action (
1.2
) in
d=2
dimensions as a high density limit of an interacting quantum Bose gas.
We recall that a quantum system of
n
spinless non-relativistic bosons in
\Lambda
is described by the Hamiltonian

\mathbb{H}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl(-\frac{\Delta_{i}}{2m}+\mathcal{U}(x_{i})\biggr)+\frac{g}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}v(x_{i}-x_{j})

acting on the subspace
L^{2}(\Lambda)^{\otimes_{s}n}
of
L^{2}(\Lambda^{n})
consisting of symmetric functions with respect to permutations. In the Hamiltonian,
\Delta_{i}
is the Laplacian acting in the variable
x_{i}
,
g
is a coupling constant,
\mathcal{U}
is a confining external potential, and
v
is a repulsive two-body interaction potential. We consider the system in the grand canonical ensemble at positive temperature, defined by the density matrix

\frac{1}{Z}\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(\mathbb{H}_{n}-\theta n)}

(1.4)

acting on the Fock space
\mathcal{F}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}L^{2}(\Lambda)^{\otimes_{s}n}
, where
\beta<\infty
is the inverse temperature,
\theta
is the chemical potential, and
Z
is a normalisation factor.

For the limiting regime that we are considering, we introduce two parameters
\nu,\varepsilon>0
, where
\nu=\frac{\beta}{m}=\sqrt{\beta g}
, and the interaction potential
v
is taken to be an approximate delta function of range
\varepsilon
.
We show that as
\nu,\varepsilon\to 0
there exists a suitable renormalisation of the chemical potential
\theta\equiv\mathfrak{h}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}
such
that the reduced density matrices of the quantum state (
1.4
) converge to the correlation functions
of the field theory (
1.1
), (
1.2
).

Previously, such a connection between (
1.1
) and (
1.4
) was obtained for
d=1
in
12
;
14
;
4
, where the short-range singularities of the field do not appear and hence no renormalisation is required. The first result of this kind in dimension larger than one was
7
, where it was established in
d=2
dimensions. This result was recently extended to
d=3
dimensions in
18
. Both of these results were established in the
homogeneous
, or translation invariant, setting, where
\Lambda
is the
d
-dimensional unit torus and
U=\mathcal{U}=0
.

The goal of this paper is to establish the connection between (
1.1
) and (
1.4
) in the
inhomogeneous
setting, where the assumption of translation invariance is dropped. Doing so is of some importance for the physical relevance of such a result. Indeed, the assumption of translation invariance does not hold in a typical experimental setup, where the Bose gas is either confined by an external trapping potential or constrained to a bounded domain with Dirichlet boundary conditions.

Forgoing translation invariance leads to significant new mathematical challenges as compared to homogeneous case. The most fundamental one is that the infinite counterterms subtracted from
V(\phi)
no longer consist of a finite collection of scalar quantities, but instead of diverging functions with nontrivial spatial dependence. It turns out that a key cancellation between such counterterms, which can be trivially seen to be exact in the homogeneous setting, fails in the inhomogeneous setting. In fact, we show that generically such a cancellation
cannot
hold as soon as the system is inhomogeneous; see (
3.8
) below. As a consequence, in the inhomogeneous setting one has to handle a new family of diverging
counterterm functions
, on both the level of the field theory (
1.1
) and the quantum many-body theory (
1.4
). We refer to Section
3
below for more details on the strategy of the proof.

As an additional ingredient of our proof, we derive precise quantitative bounds on the Green function of general Schrödinger operators on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, of its regularised version, and of its gradient. We believe that these results might be of independent interest.

We conclude this section with a summary of some related previous work. In dimensions
d\leqslant 3
, the
mean-field limit
was investigated in
13
;
15
;
6
;
4
;
5
;
23
, where the parameter
\varepsilon
was fixed as
\nu\to 0
. The resulting limiting field theory differs from
\phi^{4}_{d}
in that the interaction term
V(\phi)
is nonlocal, given by a convolution with a bounded two-body interaction potential
v
. This nonlocal interaction term is considerably less singular than the local one of
\phi^{4}_{d}
theory. The stronger singularity of
V(\phi)
requires additional renormalisation as compared to the nonlocal potential. The fundamental complications mentioned above, arising from going from the homogeneous to the inhomogeneous setting, only appear in this additional renormalisation. Accordingly, the mean-field limit was treated also in the inhomogeneous setting in
6
;
15
with minor adaptations of the proof in the homogeneous setting.

2. Setup and main results

In this section we give the precise setup and state our main results.

2.1.  The one-particle Hamiltonian

On the one-particle space
\mathcal{H}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})
we define the one-particle Hamiltonian

h\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\kappa-\frac{\Delta}{2}+U\,,

(2.1)

where
\kappa>0
,
\Delta
is the Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
U
is an external potential satisfying the following assumption.

Assumption 2.1
.

Let
d\geqslant 1
and
\theta>0
. The function
U\vcentcolon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(0,\infty)
satisfies one of the two following conditions.

(P)

U\in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and there exist constants
C,c>0
such that, for almost all
x\in{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
,

c\bigl(1+\lvert x\rvert^{\theta}\bigr)\leqslant U(x)\leqslant C\bigl(1+\lvert x\rvert^{\theta}\bigr)\,.

(2.2)

(D)

U\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and there exist a radial non-decreasing function
g\vcentcolon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[1,\infty)
and constants
C,c>0
,
0<\gamma<1
such that, for almost all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

U(x)\geqslant cg(x)\geqslant c\bigl(1+\lvert x\rvert^{\theta}\bigr)\,,

\lvert\nabla U(x)\rvert\leqslant Cg(\gamma x)^{3/2}\,.

Example 2.2
.

The following examples illustrate Assumption
2.1
.

(i)

The step potential
U(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=1+\lfloor\lvert x\rvert\rfloor^{\theta}
satisfies Assumption
2.1

(P)
, where
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor
denotes the integer part.

(ii)

For any
C>0
, the rapidly growing potential
U(x)=\exp\bigl((1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})^{C}\bigr)
satisfies Assumption
2.1

(D)
.

Remark 2.3
.

Let
d=2,3
and
s\in(d/2,2)
. Throughout this paper we shall frequently require that

\tr{h^{-s}}<\infty\,,

(2.3)

which, by the Lieb-Thierring type inequality (see e.g.
(
3
, Theorem 1)
), holds whenever
\int\mathrm{d}x\,\bigl(\kappa+U(x)\bigr)^{d/2-s}<\infty
. For
U(x)\geqslant c(1+\lvert x\rvert^{\theta})
as in Assumption
2.1
, this condition holds whenever
\theta>\frac{d}{s-d/2}
.

2.2.  Classical field theory

In this subsection we define the field theory and its correlation functions.
The classical free field
\phi
is by definition the complex-valued Gaussian field with mean zero and covariance
G\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=h^{-1}
. Explicitly, the free field may be constructed as follows.
We use the spectral decomposition
2
2

2

Note that under the condition
U(x)\geqslant c\lvert x\rvert^{\theta}
from Assumption
2.1
,
h
has a compact resolvent.

h=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}u_{k}u_{k}^{*}
, with eigenvalues
\lambda_{k}>0
and normalised eigenfunctions
u_{k}\in\mathcal{H}
. Let
X=(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of independent standard complex Gaussian random variables
3
3

3

The random variable
X
is standard complex Gaussian random variable if
\mathbb{E}X=0
,
\mathbb{E}X^{2}=0
and
\mathbb{E}\lvert X\rvert^{2}=1
.
, whose law and associated expectation are denoted by
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{E}
, respectively. The
classical free field
is then given by

\phi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{X_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\,u_{k}\,.

In order to define the interacting theory, we need to regularise the field
\phi
, which is well known to be almost surely a distribution of negative regularity. To that end, we choose a nonnegative rapidly decaying function
\vartheta\vcentcolon[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
satisfying
\vartheta(0)=1
, and for
N>0
define the regularised field

\phi_{N}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}X_{k}\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta(\lambda_{k}/N)}{\lambda_{k}}}u_{k}\,.

(2.4)

The
Green function
of the free field
\phi
is the integral kernel of
G=h^{-1}
,

G(x,y)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi(x)\bar{\phi}(y)\bigr]=\frac{1}{h}(x,y)\,.

(2.5)

Here, and throughout the following, we use the notation
A(x,y)
for the kernel of an operator
A\vcentcolon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
. Similarly, we define
G_{N}
as the Green function of regularised field
\phi_{N}

G_{N}(x,y)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\phi_{N}(x)\overline{\phi}_{N}(y)\bigr]=\frac{\vartheta(h/N)}{h}(x,y)\,.

(2.6)

Next, we suppose that
d=2
and define the regularised interaction

V_{N}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,{\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi_{N}(x)\rvert^{4}\!\vcentcolon}\,,

where
{\vcentcolon\!\cdot\!\vcentcolon}
denotes Wick ordering (see e.g.
(
7
, Appendix A)
) with respect to the measure
\mathbb{P}
. Explicitly,

{\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi_{N}(x)\rvert^{4}\!\vcentcolon}=\lvert\phi_{N}(x)\rvert^{4}-4G_{N}(x,x)\lvert\phi_{N}(x)\rvert^{2}+2(G_{N}(x,x))^{2}\,.

An application of Wick’s theorem combined with the decay estimate for
G_{N}
from Proposition
7.1
shows that, provided the lower bound of (
2.2
) holds with
\theta>2
, the regularised interaction
V_{N}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
. A similar argument shows that
V_{N}
converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P)}
. We denote its limit by
V
, which can be suggestively written as

V=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,{\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{4}\!\vcentcolon}\,.

(2.7)

The interacting complex
\phi^{4}_{2}
field theory is given by the probability measure

\frac{1}{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-V}\mathrm{d}{\mathbb{P}},\qquad\zeta\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathrm{e}^{-V}\bigr]\,.

(2.8)

Remark 2.4
.

The existence of the measure (
2.8
) is nontrivial. It amounts to the integrability condition
\zeta<\infty
. For the homogeneous
\phi^{4}_{2}
theory on the torus, this was first established in the landmark work of Nelson
20
;
19
. Nelson’s argument can be extended to the inhomogeneous case provided that the lower bound of (
2.2
) holds for large enough
\theta
. In fact, in Section
4
we prove a more general result, which establishes the uniform integrability for a family of nonlocal field theories, under the condition
\theta>10
. See also Remark
2.9
below.

The interacting field theory (
2.8
) is characterised by its correlation functions. For
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{x,\tilde{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{dp}
, we define the
p
-point correlation function
as

(\gamma_{p})_{\mathbf{x,\tilde{x}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{\zeta}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bar{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{1})\cdots\bar{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{p}){\phi}({x}_{1})\cdots{\phi}({x}_{p})\,\mathrm{e}^{-V}\Bigr]\,,

(2.9)

which is the
2p
-th moment of the field
\phi
under the probability measure (
2.8
). This measure is sub-Gaussian, and is hence determined by its moments
(\gamma_{p})_{p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
. (Note that any moment containing a different number of
\bar{\phi}
s and
\phi
s vanishes by invariance of the measure (
2.8
) under the gauge transformation
\phi\mapsto\alpha\phi
, where
\lvert\alpha\rvert=1
.)

As explained in
(
6
, Section 1.5)
, the correlation function
\gamma_{p}
is divergent on the diagonal, even for the free field. Hence, for instance, it cannot be used to analyse the distribution of the mass density
\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}
. As in
(
6
, Section 1.5)
, we remedy this issue by introducing the
Wick-ordered
p
-point correlation function

(\widehat{\gamma}_{p})_{\mathbf{x,\tilde{x}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{\zeta}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[{\vcentcolon\!\bar{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{1})\cdots\bar{\phi}(\tilde{x}_{p}){\phi}({x}_{1})\cdots{\phi}({x}_{p})\!\vcentcolon}\,\mathrm{e}^{-V}\Bigr]\,,

(2.10)

which has a regular behaviour on the diagonal. The Wick-ordered correlation function (
2.10
) can be expressed explicitly in terms of the correlation functions (
2.9
) and the correlation functions of the free field; see (
2.21
) below.

2.3.  Quantum many-body system

In this subsection we define the quantum many-body system and its reduced density matrices. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}_{n}
the orthogonal projection onto the symmetric subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
; explicitly, for
\Psi_{n}\in\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
,

\mathcal{P}_{n}\Psi_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in S_{n}}\Psi_{n}(x_{\pi(1)},\dots,x_{\pi(n)})\,,

where
S_{n}
is the group of permutations on
\{1,\dots,n\}
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we define the
n
-particle space as
\mathcal{H}_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\mathcal{P}_{n}\mathcal{H}^{\otimes n}
. We define Fock space as the Hilbert space
\mathcal{F}\equiv\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{H})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{H}_{n}
. We denote by
\Tr_{\mathcal{F}}(X)
the trace of an operator
X
acting on
\mathcal{F}
. For
f\in\mathcal{H}
we define the bosonic annihilation and creation operators
a(f)
and
a^{*}(f)
on
\mathcal{F}
through their action on a dense set of vectors
\Psi=(\Psi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{F}
as

\displaystyle\bigl(a(f)\Psi\bigr)_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n+1}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\bar{f}(x)\,\Psi_{n+1}(x,x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\,,

\displaystyle\bigl(a^{*}(f)\Psi\bigr)_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})\Psi_{n-1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n})\,.

We regard
a
and
a^{*}
as operator-valued distributions and use the customary notations

a(f)=\int\mathrm{d}x\,\bar{f}(x)a(x),\qquad\qquad a^{*}(f)=\int\mathrm{d}x\,f(x)a^{*}(x)\,.

The interaction potential of the Bose gas satisfies the following assumption.

Assumption 2.5
.

Let
v:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be an even, smooth compactly supported function of positive type
4
4

4

That is, the Fourier transform of
v
is a positive measure.
, whose integral is equal to one.

For
\varepsilon>0
we define the rescaled interaction potential

v^{\varepsilon}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{d}}v\biggl(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\,.

(2.11)

For
\varepsilon,\nu>0
, we define the interacting quantum Hamiltonian
H\equiv H_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}

H\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\nu\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,a^{*}(x)\Bigl(\kappa-\frac{\Delta}{2}+\mathcal{U}\Bigr)(x,\tilde{x})a(\tilde{x})\\
+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,a^{*}(x)a(x)\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\,a^{*}(\tilde{x})a(\tilde{x})-\nu\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}\int\mathrm{d}x\,a^{*}(x)a(x)+\mathfrak{h}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}\,,

(2.12)

a self-adjoint operator on
\mathcal{F}
. Here,
\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}
are real renormalisation parameters chosen in (
2.16
) below, so that the density matrix (
1.4
) can be expressed as

\frac{1}{Z}\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta(\mathbb{H}_{n}-\theta n)}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-H}}{Z}\,,\quad\quad Z\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\Tr_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathrm{e}^{-H})\,,

where
Z
is the grand canonical partition function. The parameters
\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}
describe a renormalisation of the chemical potential and of the energy, respectively.

For
\nu>0
, we define the free Hamiltonian
5
5

5

Note that
H^{0}
has external potential
U
(recall (
2.1
)) while
H
has external potential
\mathcal{U}
.

H^{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\nu\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,h(x,\tilde{x})\,a^{*}(x)a(\tilde{x})\,.

(2.13)

The free grand canonical partition function and the relative partition functions are respectively defined as

Z^{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\Tr_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathrm{e}^{-H^{0}})\,,\qquad\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{Z}{Z^{0}}\,.

(2.14)

In order to determine the renormalisation parameters
\mathfrak{g}_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}
, we introduce the the rescaled mean particle density in the free quantum state,

\varrho_{\nu}(x)\equiv\varrho_{\nu}^{U}({x})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\nu\Tr_{\mathcal{F}}\biggl({a^{*}(x)a(x)\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-H^{0}}}{Z^{0}}}\biggr)\,.

(2.15)

Then we set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\tau^{\varepsilon,0}+\varrho_{\nu}^{0}\,,\quad\mathfrak{h}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\varrho_{\nu}(x)v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\varrho_{\nu}(\tilde{x})+\int\mathrm{d}x\,\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)\varrho_{\nu}(x)-E^{\varepsilon}\,,

(2.16)

where

\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})G(x,\tilde{x})\,,\quad\quad E^{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\bigl(G(x,\tilde{x})\bigr)^{2}\,,

(2.17)

and we defined the constants
\tau^{\varepsilon,0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\tau^{\varepsilon}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U=0}}
and
\varrho_{\nu}^{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\varrho_{\nu}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U=0}}
which do not depend on
x
by homogeneity if
U=0
.

By the the choice (
2.15
), (
2.16
), and (
2.17
), one finds that the Hamiltonian (
2.12
) coincides with the
Wick-ordered interacting Hamiltonian

H=H^{0}+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\bigl(a^{*}(x)a(x)-\varrho_{\nu}(x)/\nu\bigr)\,v(x-\tilde{x})\,\bigl(a^{*}(\tilde{x})a(\tilde{x})-\varrho_{\nu}(\tilde{x})/\nu\bigr)\\
-\nu\int\mathrm{d}x\,\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)(a^{*}(x)a(x)-\varrho_{\nu}(x))-E^{\varepsilon}\,,

(2.18)

provided that
U\equiv U_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}>0
satisfies the equation

\mathcal{U}(x)=U(x)-(\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)-\tau^{\varepsilon,0})-v^{\varepsilon}*(\varrho_{\nu}-\varrho^{0}_{\nu})(x)\,.

(2.19)

The equation (
2.19
) relates the
bare potential

\mathcal{U}
with the
dressed, or renormalised, potential

U
. Through the dependence of
\tau^{\varepsilon}
and
\varrho_{\nu}
on
U
, it expresses the bare potential
\mathcal{U}
in terms of the dressed potential
U
. Solving
U
in terms of
\mathcal{U}
requires the solution of the nonlinear integral equation (
2.19
), known as the
counterterm problem
; it is discussed in Theorem
2.14
below.

Next, we define the
p
-particle reduced density matrix
as

(\Gamma_{p})_{\mathbf{x,\tilde{x}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\Tr_{\mathcal{F}}\biggl(a^{*}(\tilde{x}_{1})\cdots a^{*}(\tilde{x}_{p})a(x_{1})\cdots a(x_{p})\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-H}}{Z}\biggr)\,.

(2.20)

As for the correlation function (
2.9
) and its Wick-ordered version (
2.10
), we would like to replace (
2.20
) with its Wick-ordered version. To that end, we regard the expressions (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) as integral kernels of operators acting on
\mathcal{H}_{p}
, and observe that (see
(
6
, Lemma A.4)
)

\widehat{\gamma}_{p}=\sum_{k=0}^{p}\binom{p}{k}^{2}(-1)^{p-k}\mathcal{P}_{p}\bigl(\gamma_{k}\otimes\gamma_{p-k}^{0}\bigr)\mathcal{P}_{p}\,,

(2.21)

where
\gamma^{0}_{p}
is the correlation function of the free field.
By analogy with (
2.21
), we define the
Wick-ordered reduced density matrix
as

\widehat{\Gamma}_{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{p}\binom{p}{k}^{2}(-1)^{p-k}\mathcal{P}_{p}\bigl(\Gamma_{k}\otimes\Gamma_{p-k}^{0}\bigr)\mathcal{P}_{p}\,,

(2.22)

where
\Gamma_{m}^{0}
is the
m
-particle reduced density matrix of the free grand canonical density matrix
\mathrm{e}^{-H^{0}}/Z^{0}
.

2.4.  Results

The following is our main result.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
d=2
and
\theta>10
. Let
U
satisfy Assumption
2.1
, and
H
be the Hamiltonian (
2.18
).
Suppose that Assumption
2.5
holds and
\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon(\nu)
satisfies

\varepsilon\geqslant\exp\Bigl(-\bigl(\log\nu^{-1}\bigr)^{\frac{1-c}{2}}\Bigr)

(2.23)

for some
c>0
.
Then as
\varepsilon,\nu\to 0
we have the convergence of the partition function

\mathcal{Z}\to\zeta

(2.24)

and of the Wick-ordered correlation functions

\nu^{p}\,\widehat{\Gamma}_{p}\xrightarrow{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}\widehat{\gamma}_{p}

(2.25)

for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
.

The topology of convergence in (
2.25
) is strong enough to address most questions of physical interest, regarding both small-scale (ultraviolet) behaviour as well as large-scale (infrared) behaviour. For instance, it allows for an analysis of arbitrary correlation functions of the renormalised particle densities; see Corollary
2.8
below.

Combining Theorem
2.6
with the behaviour of the Green function from Proposition
7.1
below, arguing as in
(
6
, Theorem 1.3)
, we obtain the following result. It gives convergence of the non-renormalised reduced density matrices, with optimal range of
L^{r}
exponents.

Corollary 2.7
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.6
, for every
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leqslant r<\infty
,

\nu^{p}\,\Gamma_{p}\xrightarrow{L^{r}}\gamma_{p}

Another consequence of Theorem
2.6
is the convergence in law of the of the renormalised particle densities, similarly to
(
6
, Theorem 1.4)
. To state the result, we define the
quantum particle density
and the
classical mass density
, respectively

\mathfrak{N}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=a^{*}(x)a(x)\,,\qquad\mathfrak{n}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=|\phi(x)|^{2}\,.

Their renormalised versions are defined by
6
6

6

For
d=2
, the mass density
\mathfrak{n}(x)
is ill-defined, but its renormalized version
{\vcentcolon\!\mathfrak{n}(x)\!\vcentcolon}
is well-defined; the latter is constructed as the
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
-limit of its regularisation, like (
2.7
).

{\vcentcolon\!\mathfrak{N}(x)\!\vcentcolon}=\mathfrak{N}(x)-\Tr_{\mathcal{F}}\biggl(\mathfrak{N}(x)\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-H^{0}}}{Z^{0}}\biggr)\,,\qquad{\vcentcolon\!\mathfrak{n}(x)\!\vcentcolon}=\mathfrak{n}(x)-\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{n}(x)]\,.

Corollary 2.8
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.6
, for every
p\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
1\leqslant r<\infty
, as
\varepsilon,\nu\to 0
,

\Tr_{\mathcal{F}}\biggl(\nu^{p}\,{\vcentcolon\!\mathfrak{N}(x_{1})\!\vcentcolon}\cdots{\vcentcolon\!\mathfrak{N}(x_{p})\!\vcentcolon}\,\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-H}}{Z}\biggr)\xrightarrow{L^{r}}\frac{1}{\zeta}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[{\vcentcolon\!|\phi(x_{1})|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\cdots{\vcentcolon\!|\phi(x_{p})|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\,\mathrm{e}^{-V(\phi)}\Bigr]\,.

The convergence is also locally uniform for distinct arguments
x_{1},\dots,x_{p}
.

Remark 2.9
.

Our results hold under the assumption
\theta>10
. This restriction is technical and can be improved, for instance under stronger assumptions on the smoothness of
U
; see Remark
4.6
below.

Remark 2.10
.

The lower bound on the interaction length from (
2.23
) is the same as in the homogeneous result from
7
. In the homogeneous setting, it was recently improved in
18
;
11
to
\varepsilon\geqslant\nu^{c}
for a sufficiently small constant
c>0
, at the expense of a weaker topology of convergence in (
2.25
) (weak operator convergence against test functions with finite Fourier support.)

Finally, we discuss the
counterterm problem
(
2.19
), which relates the bare potential
\mathcal{U}
with the dressed potential
U
, and hence the Hamiltonian (
2.12
) with its Wick-ordered formulation (
2.18
). We make the following assumption on the bare potential
\mathcal{U}
.

Assumption 2.11
.

Fix
\theta>2
. There exist a radially increasing function
g\vcentcolon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[1,\infty)
and constants
C,c,b>0
,
\gamma\in(0,1)
satisfying

c\lvert x\rvert^{\theta}\leqslant g(x)\leqslant C\mathrm{e}^{c\lvert x\rvert^{b}}\,

(2.26)

and

cg(x)\leqslant\mathcal{U}(x)\leqslant Cg^{3/2}(\gamma x)\,

(2.27)

for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Remark 2.12
.

If
\mathcal{U}
satisfies Assumption
2.1

(D)
then (
2.26
) implies (
2.27
). Indeed, in that case

\mathcal{U}(x)\leqslant C+\bigg|{\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\lambda\,\nabla\mathcal{U}(\lambda x)\cdot x}\bigg|\leqslant C+Cg^{3/2}(\gamma x)\cdot\lvert x\rvert\leqslant Cg^{3/2}(\tilde{\gamma}x)\quad\text{a.e.}

for any
\tilde{\gamma}\in(\gamma,1)
and some constant
C
.

Our main result on the counterterm problem, Theorem
2.14
below, is the solvability of (
2.19
) in
U
for a given
\mathcal{U}
. It is a generalisation of the analogous result in
(
4
, Theorem 5.2 and Remark 5.3)
and
(
6
, Proposition 1.15)
. It expands on these works in four main directions. First, it goes beyond the mean-field regime, where
\varepsilon
is fixed, to short-range interactions, where
\varepsilon\to 0
. Second, we have to deal with the additional term
\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)-\tau^{\varepsilon,0}
in (
2.19
), which arises from the stronger renormalisation for local interactions. Third, we control the gradient of the dressed potential, uniformly in
\nu
and
\varepsilon
. Fourth, we relax the condition of subexponential growth for
\mathcal{U}
in the
4
;
6
to the weaker condition (
2.26
). The former three are important for our application in Theorem
2.15
below.

For the solvability of (
2.19
) in
U
, we require
\kappa
to be large enough, which is necessary as the following remark explains.

Remark 2.13
.

By the Feynman-Kac formula (Lemma
5.11
below), the functions
\tau^{\varepsilon,0}-\tau^{\varepsilon}
and
\varrho^{0}_{\nu}-\varrho_{\nu}
appearing on the right-hand side of (
2.19
) are positive whenever
v\geqslant 0
, and they are decreasing with
\kappa>0
. For a given
\mathcal{U}>0
, since
U
has to be also positive, we therefore require the above positive terms to be small enough, which imposes a lower bound on
\kappa
. (Note that, since these two terms are bounded while
U
and
\mathcal{U}
grow at infinity, this restriction arises from the behaviour of
U
and
\mathcal{U}
near the origin.)

Theorem 2.14
.

Let
d=2
. Let
\mathcal{U}
and
g
satisfy Assumption
2.11
and
v
satisfy Assumption
2.5
.
Then, for
\kappa>0
large enough, the following holds.

(i)

The counterterm problem (
2.19
) has a unique solution
U_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}
for every
\varepsilon,\nu>0
.

(ii)

Suppose that
\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon(\nu)>0
satisfies

\varepsilon(\nu)>\nu^{\beta}

(2.28)

with
0\leqslant\beta<\frac{2-s}{6}
and
s
satisfying (
2.3
). There exists a unique positive function
U
satisfying the limiting counterterm problem

\mathcal{U}(x)=U(x)-2\biggl(\frac{1}{\kappa-\Delta/2+U}-\frac{1}{\kappa-\Delta/2}\biggr)(x,x)\,.

(2.29)

It satisfies

\lim_{\varepsilon,\nu\to 0}\biggl\lVert\frac{U_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}-U}{\mathcal{U}}\biggr\rVert_{L^{\infty}}=0\,.

(iii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathcal{U}/C<U_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}<C\mathcal{U}\,.

(iv)

If
\mathcal{U}
satisfies
(D)
in Assumption
2.1
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\lvert\nabla U_{\nu}^{\varepsilon}(x)\rvert\vee\lvert\nabla U(x)\rvert\leqslant Cg(\gamma x)^{3/2}\,,

and
U^{\varepsilon}_{\nu},U
satisfy
(D)
for all
\nu,\varepsilon>0
.

We remark that a similar statement holds in dimension
d=3
, with a similar proof; we omit the details. Combining Theorem
2.14
with Theorem
2.6
easily yields the following result.

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
d=2
and
\theta>10
. Let the bare potential
\mathcal{U}
satisfy Assumption
2.1
and
2.11
. Let
v
satisfy Assumption
2.5
and
\varepsilon>0
satisfy (
2.23
). Let
\kappa>0
be large enough. Let
H
be the Hamiltonian (
2.12
) with renormalisation parameters
\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\varepsilon}_{\nu}
defined in (
2.16
).
Then there exists a dressed potential
U
which is the unique solution of (
2.29
), such that (
2.24
) and (
2.25
) hold.

In this result, the bare potential
\mathcal{U}
is the external potential of the quantum theory, while the dressed potential
U
is the external potential of the limiting field theory.

3. Strategy of the proof

The rest of the paper is devoted to the proofs of Theorems
2.6
and
2.14
. In this section we describe the general strategy of the proof. Throughout the paper we use
C,c
to denote generic positive constants, which may change from one line to another, and may depend on fixed parameters. We write
a\lesssim b
to mean
a\leqslant Cb
. If
C
depends on a parameter
\alpha
, we write
a\lesssim_{\alpha}b
. To emphasise the dependence of a quantity
X
on the potential
U
or on the interaction
V
, we include these parameters as a superscript and write
X^{U}
or
X^{V}
, respectively. For a positive-definite operator
A
, we denote by
\mu_{A}
the centred Gaussian measure with covariance
A
.

Our general strategy is the two-step approach introduced in
7
. As in that paper, we introduce two nonlocal interactions approximating
V
from (
2.7
):

\displaystyle V^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\,{\vcentcolon\!|\phi(x)|^{2}|\phi(\tilde{x})|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\,,

(3.1)

\displaystyle W^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,{\vcentcolon\!|\phi(x)|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\,{\vcentcolon\!|\phi(\tilde{x})|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}-\int\mathrm{d}x\,\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)\,{\vcentcolon\!|\phi(x)|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}-E^{\varepsilon}\,,

(3.2)

which are rigorously constructed by regularisation, as in (
2.7
).

The first step consists in estimating the difference between the relative partition function
\mathcal{Z}
and the classical partition function
\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}
, as well as between the Wick-ordered reduced density matrices
\nu^{p}\widehat{\Gamma}_{p}
and the Wick-ordered correlation functions
\widehat{\gamma}_{p}^{W^{\varepsilon}}
.
The second step establishes the convergence of
\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}
and
\widehat{\gamma}_{p}^{W^{\varepsilon}}
to
\zeta^{V}
and
\widehat{\gamma}_{p}^{V}
, respectively, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, via the intermediate potential
V^{\varepsilon}
.

These two steps are the subject of the two following propositions, which are the counterparts of
(
7
, Proposition 3.1 and 3.2)
, respectively.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
d=2,3
, and let
\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon(\nu)\geqslant 0
satisfy

\varepsilon(\nu)\geqslant\begin{cases}\exp\Bigl(-\bigl(\log\nu^{-1}\bigr)^{\frac{1-c}{2}}\Bigr)\quad&{\rm if}\,d=2\\
(\log\nu^{-1})^{-\frac{1-c}{2}}\quad&{\rm if}\,d=3\end{cases}

(3.3)

for some
c\in(0,1)
.
Then, under the assumptions of Theorem
2.6
,

\lim_{\varepsilon,\nu\to 0}\lvert\mathcal{Z}-\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}\rvert=0\,.

Moreover, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon,\nu\to 0}\big\|\nu^{p}\widehat{\Gamma}_{p}-\widehat{\gamma}_{p}^{W^{\varepsilon}}\big\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}=0\,.

(3.4)

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
d=2
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.6
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}\to\zeta\,.

(3.5)

Moreover, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\big\|\widehat{\gamma}_{p}-\widehat{\gamma}_{p}^{W^{\varepsilon}}\big\|_{L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}}=0\,.

(3.6)

Going to an unbounded domain and giving up translation invariance results in major differences in the proofs as compared to
7
, some resulting from fundamentally new difficulties which simply do not appear in the homogeneous setting. In the rest of this section, we sketch some of these differences.

The first step, corresponding to Proposition
3.1
, relies on a functional integral representation for the quantum and classical problems, derived in
6
and also used in
7
. To explain this representation, we focus on the partition function
\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}
which can be formally written as

\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}=\frac{1}{\int\mathrm{D}\phi\,\mathrm{e}^{-\langle\phi\mspace{2mu},h\phi\rangle}}\int\mathrm{D}\phi\,\mathrm{e}^{-\langle\phi\mspace{2mu},h\phi\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,:\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}:\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\,:\lvert\phi(\tilde{x})\rvert^{2}:+\int\mathrm{d}x\,\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)\,:|\phi(x)|^{2}:+E^{\varepsilon}}\,.

We rewrite the interaction term
-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,{\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\,{\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi(\tilde{x})\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}
using a Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation

\int\mu_{v^{\varepsilon}}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\langle f,\xi\rangle}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\langle f,v^{\varepsilon}f\rangle}\,,

(3.7)

where
\mu_{v^{\varepsilon}}
is a real centred Gaussian measure with covariance
\int\mu_{v^{\varepsilon}}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\,\xi(x)\,\xi(y)=v^{\varepsilon}(x-y)
. Choosing
f(x)={\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}
and interchanging the order of integration, we obtain

\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}=\frac{1}{\int\mathrm{D}\phi\,\mathrm{e}^{-\langle\phi\mspace{2mu},h\phi\rangle}}\int\mu_{v^{\varepsilon}}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\int\mathrm{D}\phi\,\mathrm{e}^{-\langle\phi\mspace{2mu},h\phi\rangle+\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\xi(x)\,:\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}:+\int\mathrm{d}x\,\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)\,:|\phi(x)|^{2}:+E^{\varepsilon}}\,.

The advantage of this representation is that the integration over
\phi
has become Gaussian, at the expense of introducing the auxiliary Gaussian field
\xi
. However, the integral over
\phi
on the right-hand side is badly divergent owing to the rapid quadratic growth of the positive term
\int\mathrm{d}x\,\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)\,:|\phi(x)|^{2}:
, which overwhelms the negative term
-\langle\phi\mspace{2mu},h\phi\rangle
for small
\varepsilon
(it is easy to see that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)=+\infty
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
). Thus, the interchange of the order of integration over
\xi
and
\phi
is not allowed owing to strong oscillations in the integral over
\phi
.

To remedy this issue, we perform the Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation (
3.7
) with the shifted phase
f(x)={\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}-\alpha(x)
for some suitably chosen real function
\alpha
, and obtain

\displaystyle\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{E^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\alpha(x)v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\alpha(\tilde{x})}}{\int\mathrm{D}\phi\,\mathrm{e}^{-\langle\phi\mspace{2mu},h\phi\rangle}}\int\mu_{v^{\varepsilon}}(\mathrm{d}\xi)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int\mathrm{D}\phi\,\exp\biggl(-\langle\phi\mspace{2mu},h\phi\rangle+\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\xi(x)\,({\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}-\alpha(x))-\int\mathrm{d}x\,q^{\varepsilon}(x)\,{\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{E^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\alpha(x)v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\alpha(\tilde{x})}\int\mu_{v^{\varepsilon}}(\mathrm{d}\xi)\,\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathrm{e}^{-\langle\phi\mspace{2mu},h\phi\rangle+\mathrm{i}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\xi(x)\,(:\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}:-\alpha(x))-\int\mathrm{d}x\,q^{\varepsilon}(x)\,:\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}:}\Bigr]

where we abbreviated

q^{\varepsilon}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\alpha(\tilde{x})-\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)\,.

The term involving
q^{\varepsilon}
is potentially dangerous, since, as explained above, it can become very large and hence overwhelm the quadratic decay provided by the term
-\langle\phi\mspace{2mu},h\phi\rangle
. Its vanishing is equivalent to
v^{\varepsilon}*\alpha^{\varepsilon}=\tau^{\varepsilon}
, i.e.

\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\,\alpha(\tilde{x})=\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\,G(x,\tilde{x})\,.

(3.8)

In the
homogeneous case
, where
G(x,\tilde{x})=G(x-\tilde{x})
, solving (
3.8
) is trivial since
\alpha
can simply be chosen as a constant.

On the other hand, whenever
G
is not translation invariant, (
3.8
) generically has no solution for any
\varepsilon>0
; the basic heuristic is that its right-hand side is much rougher than its left-hand side (which is smooth by smoothness of
v^{\varepsilon}
). As an illustration of this phenomenon, in Appendix
A
we show the non-existence of solutions to (
3.8
) for any discontinuous locally bounded potentials
U
and any positive interaction
v
satisfying Assumption
2.5
.

Our basic strategy in the inhomogeneous setting, where
q^{\varepsilon}
cannot be chosen to vanish, is to choose the function
\alpha
in a suitable way so that the integral over
\phi
remains well-defined, and to absorb the function
q^{\varepsilon}
into the external potential
U
in the Hamiltonian
h
. This latter step results in a modified,
\varepsilon
-dependent, external potential, which results in a modified
\varepsilon
-dependent covariance for the classical field theory. As a consequence, we have to re-Wick-order all diverging quantities after the change of covariance.

Let us explain a bit more carefully how this strategy is implemented. In fact, owing to the growth of the potential
U
inside
h
in its argument
x
, it is sufficient to construct
\alpha
such that

\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\,\alpha^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})-\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)\geqslant 0

holds only in a ball of sufficiently large radius (depending on
\varepsilon
). Since
\tau^{\varepsilon}
decays in
x
for any fixed
\varepsilon
by the decay of
G
, this can be done by choosing
\alpha\equiv\alpha^{\varepsilon}
supported in a large enough ball.

Denoting
U^{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=U+q^{\varepsilon}
, we therefore obtain, for every
\varepsilon>0
, a modified one-particle Hamiltonian

h^{U^{\varepsilon}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\kappa-\frac{\Delta}{2}+U^{\varepsilon}

satisfying the conditions in Remark
2.3
. This results in an
\varepsilon
-dependent tilting of the Gaussian measure
\mathbb{P}
, according to the elementary formula

\mathrm{e}^{-\langle z,Bz\rangle}\mu_{A^{-1}}(\mathrm{d}z)=\mu_{(A+B)^{-1}}(\mathrm{d}z)\int\mathrm{e}^{-\langle y,By\rangle}\mu_{A^{-1}}(\mathrm{d}y)

for a finite-dimensional Gaussian measure
\mu_{A^{-1}}
with covariance
A
(change of covariance and correction by multiplicative factor). This tilting is rigorously performed by truncation of
\mathbb{P}
to restrict it to finite-dimensional measures.

Having tilted the free Gaussian measure
\mathbb{P}
with covariance
h^{-1}
, we have to adapt the Wick-ordering of the mass
{\lvert\phi(x)\rvert}^{2}
with respect to the new measure with covariance
(h^{U^{\varepsilon}})^{-1}
. This yields

\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}=\mathcal{C}\mathrm{e}^{E^{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\alpha^{\varepsilon}(x)v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\alpha^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})}\\
\times\mathbb{E}^{U^{\varepsilon}}\Bigl[\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\bigl(|\phi(x)|^{2}-G^{U^{\varepsilon}}(x,x)-\alpha^{\varepsilon}(x)-{T}^{\varepsilon}(x)\bigr)v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\bigl(|\phi(\tilde{x})|^{2}-G^{U^{\varepsilon}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x})-\alpha^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})-{T}^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})\bigr)}\Bigr]

(3.9)

with

\mathcal{C}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathrm{e}^{-\int\mathrm{d}x\,\bigl(\alpha^{\varepsilon}(x)-\tau^{\varepsilon}(x)\bigr){\bigl(\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}}-G^{U}(x,x)\bigr)}\Bigr]

(3.10)

and

T^{\varepsilon}(x)=G^{U}(x,x)-G^{U^{\varepsilon}}(x,x)\,.

(3.11)

As in
6
, the derivation above is achieved rigorously by introducing a finite-dimensional regularisation of the field
\phi
, and adding a spatial cutoff to the interaction. To that end, we choose an even, compactly supported mollifier
\varphi
that is positive definite and satisfies
\varphi(0)=1
. Moreover, we choose the regularisation function
\vartheta
in (
2.4
) as

\vartheta(x)=\mathbbold{1}_{\lvert x\rvert\leqslant 1}
.
The regularised, finite dimensional version of the interaction
W^{\varepsilon}
is defined as

W^{\varepsilon}_{\eta,N}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,{\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi_{N}({x})\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\,\varphi(\eta x)v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\varphi(\eta\tilde{x})\,{\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}-\int\mathrm{d}x\,\tau^{\varepsilon}(x){\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi_{N}({x})\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}-E^{\varepsilon}

(3.12)

for any
N>0
and
\eta\geqslant 0
.

These steps require a careful treatment of the convergence of the finite dimensional counterparts of (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) to the infinite dimensional case. More specifically, we perform the Hubbard-Stratonovich transformation described above in the regularised, finite-dimensional version of
W^{\varepsilon}
, and thus obtain a functional integral representation where the external potential

U^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=U-\alpha^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}+\tau^{\varepsilon}

(3.13)

depends on
\varepsilon,\eta>0
, and we set

\alpha^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\int\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\alpha^{\varepsilon}(\tilde{x})\,\varphi(\eta x)v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\varphi(\eta\tilde{x})\,.

(3.14)

This concludes the overview of the functional integral representation for
\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}
in the inhomogeneous setting.

In order to establish the connection to the quantum many-body system and its associated functional integral representations, we have to derive and control a representation analogous to the one described above in the quantum setting. This requires a suitable adaptation of the space-time functional integral from
6
, in a form that we can relate to (
3.9
). We show that the quantum terms arising from this algebraic transformation converge to their classical counterparts in (
3.9
). For this purpose, we need quantitative estimates on the difference of these quantities as
\nu,\varepsilon\to 0
. These estimates are generalisations of the results of
7
and provide stronger bounds compared to previous works.

The second step, in analogy to
7
, consists in showing integrability of
\mathrm{e}^{-V^{\varepsilon}}
, uniformly in
\varepsilon
, to deduce integrability of
\mathrm{e}^{-W^{\varepsilon}}
using hypercontractivity. Moreover, we use Gaussian integration by parts, to
derive a representation of the correlation functions in terms of expectations of derivatives of the
interaction potential.
The main challenge in this step consists in strong bounds on the decay and regularity of the (truncated) Green function of the operator
h=\kappa-\Delta/2+U
.

Outline of the paper

We conclude this section with an outline of the paper. In Section
4
we show Proposition
3.2
, while in Section
5
we develope the functional integral representation described above. In Section
6
we prove Proposition
3.1
. In Section
7
we show properties of the Green function which are crucial to show Proposition
3.2
. Finally, in Section
8
analyse the counterterm problem and prove Theorem
2.14
.

4. Proof of Proposition
3.2

This section is dedicated to the proof of Proposition
3.2
; it is the analogue of
(
7
, Section 4)
in the inhomogeneous setting. We derive first
L^{2}
-estimates for the differences
V^{\varepsilon}-V
and
V^{\varepsilon}-W^{\varepsilon}
. Then we show integrability of
\mathrm{e}^{-V^{\varepsilon}}
, which also implies the integrability of
\mathrm{e}^{-W^{\varepsilon}}
.
Thus we show the convergence of the partition functions (
3.5
) and of the correlation functions (
3.6
).

Throughout this section we assume that
d=2
. We abbreviate
L^{2}\equiv L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
. Moreover,
g
is a radially increasing lower bound (up to a multiplicative constant) of the potential
U
. In the case where
U
satisfies Assumption
2.1

(P)
,
g(x)=1+\lvert x\rvert^{\theta}
. For fixed
0<\gamma<1
, we also define

\tilde{g}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=g(\gamma x)\,.

(4.1)

4.1.  Partition function

We start by deriving
L^{2}
-estimates for the differences
V^{\varepsilon}-W^{\varepsilon}
and
V^{\varepsilon}-V
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Consider
V^{\varepsilon}
and
W^{\varepsilon}
as in (
3.1
) and (
3.2
). Let
\theta>2
. Then

\|V^{\varepsilon}-W^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\to 0\quad\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0\,.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the one of
7
. The main novelty involves controlling the large-
x
regime, which is done using the Green function estimates from Section
7
.

A straightforward computation shows that

\begin{split}W^{\varepsilon}-V^{\varepsilon}&=\int\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})G(x,\tilde{x})\bigl({\vcentcolon\!\overline{\phi}(x)\phi(\tilde{x})\!\vcentcolon}-{\vcentcolon\!\lvert\phi(x)\rvert^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\bigr)\,\\
&=\,(W^{\varepsilon}-V^{\varepsilon})_{R}+(W^{\varepsilon}-V^{\varepsilon})_{R^{c}}\,,\end{split}

where we split the difference
W^{\varepsilon}-V^{\varepsilon}
in two regions: inside and outside of a ball of radius
R=R(\varepsilon)
. We analyse the two terms separately, using different arguments. To estimate the term inside the ball, we can employ a-priori bounds on the gradient of Green’s function, whereas outside the ball, we rely on its exponential decay.
Let us consider first the region
|x|\leqslant R
. Then using Wick ordering formula and since
G(x;y)=\int\mathrm{d}\mu\,\overline{\phi}(y)\phi(x)
we have

\begin{split}\|(V^{\varepsilon}-W^{\varepsilon})_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}&=\int_{|x|,|y|\leqslant R}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})G(x,\tilde{x})G(y,\tilde{y})\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\bigl(G(\tilde{y},x)-G(y,x)\bigr)\bigl(G(\tilde{x},y)-G(x,y)\bigr)\,.\\
\end{split}

Hence, we use the mean value theorem to rewrite the difference
G(\tilde{y},x)-G(y,x)
and
G(\tilde{x},y)-G(x,y)
as

\big|G(x,y)-G(x,z)\big|\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\lambda\,|y-z||\nabla_{y}G(x,y+(z-y)\lambda)|\,.

(4.2)

Together with the bound (
7.5
), in Appendix
7
, we have

\begin{split}&\|(V^{\varepsilon}-W^{\varepsilon})_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\int_{|x|,|y|\leqslant R}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})G(x,\tilde{x})G(y,\tilde{y})\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\lambda\,\frac{|\tilde{y}-y|}{|\tilde{y}+(y-\tilde{y})\lambda-x|}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,\frac{|\tilde{x}-x|}{|\tilde{x}+(x-\tilde{x})s-y|}\\
&\lesssim\int_{|x|,|y|\leqslant R}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v(h)v(k)G(x,\varepsilon h+x)G(y,\varepsilon k+y)\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\times\Big|\log\frac{|x-y+\varepsilon k|}{|x-y|}\Big|\Big|\log\frac{|y-x+\varepsilon h|}{|y-x|}\Big|\end{split}

(4.3)

where in the last line, after integrating, we used the change of variable
h=(x-\tilde{x})/\varepsilon
and
k=(y-\tilde{y})/\varepsilon
. To bound the term in the last line on the right-hand side in (
4.3
) we use on one side
G(x,y)|x-y|\leqslant C_{\alpha}|x-y|^{\alpha}\lesssim_{\alpha}|x-y|^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
, which is a consequence of Proposition
7.1
and on the other the bound (as in
7
)

\Big|\log\frac{|x+y|}{|x|}\Big|\lesssim\begin{cases}|\log|x+y||+|\log|x||\quad&\text{if }|x|\leqslant 2|y|\\
\frac{|y|}{|x|}\quad\quad&\text{if }|x|>2|y\,|.\end{cases}

(4.4)

Thus for
0<\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha<1
we have

\begin{split}&\int_{|x|,|y|\leqslant R}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v(h)v(k)G(x,\varepsilon h+x)G(y,\varepsilon k+y)\Big|\log\frac{|x-y+\varepsilon k|}{|x-y|}\Big|\Big|\log\frac{|y-x+\varepsilon h|}{|y-x|}\Big|\\
&\lesssim\int_{|x|,|y|\leqslant R}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v(h)v(k)G(x,\varepsilon h+x)G(y,\varepsilon k+y)\varepsilon^{\alpha}|h|^{\alpha}\varepsilon^{\alpha}|k|^{\alpha}\mathbbm{1}_{|x-y|\leqslant 2\varepsilon\min(|k|,|h|)}\\
&\quad+\int_{|x|,|y|\leqslant R}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v(h)v(k)G(x,\varepsilon h+x)G(y,\varepsilon k+y)2\frac{\varepsilon^{\alpha}|h|^{\alpha}\varepsilon|k|}{|x-y|}\mathbbm{1}_{2\varepsilon|h|\geqslant|x-y|>2\varepsilon|k|}\\
&\quad+\int_{|x|,|y|\leqslant R}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v(h)v(k)G(x,\varepsilon h+x)G(y,\varepsilon k+y)\frac{\varepsilon|k|\varepsilon|h|}{|y-x|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|x-y|>2\varepsilon\max(|h|,|k|)}\\
&\lesssim R^{2}\varepsilon^{2\alpha^{\prime}}\,,\end{split}

(4.5)

where in the last step we used

\begin{split}&\int_{|x|,|y|\leqslant R}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,G(x,\varepsilon h+x)G(y,\varepsilon k+y)\frac{\varepsilon|k|\varepsilon|h|}{|y-x|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{|x-y|>2\varepsilon\max(|h|,|k|)}\\
&\lesssim\int_{|x|,|y|\leqslant R}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\frac{(\varepsilon|k|\varepsilon|h|)^{\tilde{\alpha}}}{|y-x|^{2\alpha}}\mathbbm{1}_{|x-y|>2\varepsilon\max(|h|,|k|)}\lesssim(\varepsilon|k|\varepsilon|h|)^{\alpha^{\prime}}R^{2}\,.\end{split}

Next, we consider the region outside the ball, i.e.
|x|>R
. Moreover, we consider the two terms appearing in
(W^{\varepsilon}-V^{\varepsilon})_{R^{c}}
proportional to
{\vcentcolon\!\overline{\phi}(x)\phi(\tilde{x})\!\vcentcolon}
and
{\vcentcolon\!|\phi(x)|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}
separately.

On one side, performing a similar change of variables as above and using the bound (
7.2
) we have

\begin{split}&\Big\|\int_{|x|>R}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})G(x,\tilde{x}){\vcentcolon\!|\phi(x)|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\Big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\quad\lesssim\int_{|x|,|y|>R}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v(h)v(k)G(x,x+\varepsilon h)G(y,y+\varepsilon k)G(y,x)^{2}\\
&\quad\lesssim\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v(h)v(k)(\log(\varepsilon|h|)^{-1}\vee 1)(\log(\varepsilon|k|)^{-1}\vee 1)\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt\times\bigl((\log|x-y|^{-1})^{2}\vee 1\bigr)\mathrm{e}^{-c(|x-y|)\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)+\tilde{g}(y)}}\\
&\quad\lesssim\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R\\
|x-y|\leqslant 1\end{subarray}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]\bigl[(\log|x-y|^{-1})^{2}\vee 1\bigr]\mathrm{e}^{-c|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)+\tilde{g}(y)}}\\
&\quad\quad+\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R\\
|x-y|>1\end{subarray}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]\bigl[(\log|x-y|^{-1})^{2}\vee 1\bigr]\mathrm{e}^{-c|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)+\tilde{g}(y)}}\,.\end{split}

(4.6)

Now, for the region where
|x-y|>1
the integral is controlled by

\begin{split}&\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R\\
|x-y|>1\end{subarray}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]\bigl[(\log|x-y|^{-1})^{2}\vee 1\bigr]\mathrm{e}^{-c|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)+\tilde{g}(y)}}\\
&\quad\lesssim\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R\\
|x-y|>1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]\mathrm{e}^{-c(\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)}+\sqrt{\tilde{g}(y)})}\lesssim\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]R^{-10}\,.\end{split}

As for
|x-y|\leqslant 1
, we have

\begin{split}&\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R\\
|x-y|\leqslant 1\end{subarray}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]\bigl[(\log|x-y|^{-1})^{2}\vee 1\bigr]\mathrm{e}^{-c|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)}}\\
&\quad\lesssim\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|>R\\
\end{subarray}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\frac{1}{\tilde{g}(x)}\int_{|z|\leqslant\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)}}\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{e}^{-c|z|}\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\Bigl(\frac{\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)}}{|z|}\Bigr)\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]\\
&\quad\lesssim\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|>R\\
\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}x\,\frac{1}{\tilde{g}(x)}(\log(\tilde{g}(x))^{2}\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]\lesssim R^{-c}\Bigl[\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\vee 1\Bigr]\end{split}

where the last step is justified by Assumption
2.1
on
U
and thus on
g
.

On the other side, if we consider the term proportional to
{\vcentcolon\!\overline{\phi}(x)\phi(\tilde{x})\!\vcentcolon}
and using the fact that
v
has compact support, we have

\begin{split}&\Big\|\int_{|x|>R}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})G(x,\tilde{x})\,{\vcentcolon\!\overline{\phi}(x)\phi(\tilde{x})\!\vcentcolon}\Big\|_{L^{2}}\\
&\quad\lesssim\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R-1\\
|\tilde{x}|,|\tilde{y}|>R-1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})G(x,\tilde{x})G(y,\tilde{y})G(x,y)G(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\\
&\quad\lesssim\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R-1\\
|\tilde{x}|,|\tilde{y}|>R-1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})G(x,\tilde{x})G(y,\tilde{y})\bigl(G(x,y)^{2}+G(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})^{2}\bigr)\\
\end{split}

which can be bounded analogously as in (
4.6
). Putting together the bounds for
(W^{\varepsilon}-V^{\varepsilon})_{R},(W^{\varepsilon}-V^{\varepsilon})_{R^{c}}
, and optimizing over
R=R(\varepsilon)
, we can conclude for some
\tilde{c},c>0

\|V^{\varepsilon}-W^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim R^{\tilde{c}}\varepsilon^{c}+R^{-c}\Bigl(\log\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon^{c/2}\to 0\quad\text{as }\quad\varepsilon\to 0\,.\qed

Now we compare the difference
V^{\varepsilon}-V
in
L^{2}
-norm. The proof is on the same line as the one above. We have the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\theta>2
. Let
V
and
V^{\varepsilon}
defined as in (
2.7
), (
3.1
). Then we have

\|V^{\varepsilon}-V\|_{L^{2}}\to 0\quad\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0\,.

Proof.

Proceeding similarly as in the proof of Lemma
4.1
we consider
V^{\varepsilon}-V
inside and outside a ball of radius
R
. Clearly we have,

\begin{split}V^{\varepsilon}-V&=\frac{1}{2}\int\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\bigl(v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})-\delta(x-\tilde{x})\bigr){\vcentcolon\!|\phi(x)|^{2}|\phi(\tilde{x})|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\\
&=\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}(V^{\varepsilon}-V)_{R}+(V^{\varepsilon}-V)_{R^{c}}\,.\end{split}

(4.7)

The term
(V^{\varepsilon}-V)_{R}
can be estimated as
(W^{\varepsilon}-V^{\varepsilon})_{R}
(compare also with
(
7
, Lemma 4.2)
), and in particular using the Wick formula (cf.
(
7
, Appendix A)
) we have,

\begin{split}\|(V-V^{\varepsilon})_{R}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{|x|,|y|<R}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,\bigl(v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})-\delta(x-\tilde{x})\bigr)\bigl(v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})-\delta(y-\tilde{y})\bigr)\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times G(y,x)G(\tilde{y},\tilde{x})\bigl[G(x,y)G(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})+G(x,\tilde{y})G(\tilde{x},y)\bigr]\,.\\
\end{split}

We analyse only the first term on the right-hand side; the second one can be bounded similarly. Using the change of variables
\tilde{x}-x=h
and
\tilde{y}-y=k
, and that
\int v=1
we have

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\int_{|x|,|y|<R}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,G(y,x)^{2}\int\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v^{\varepsilon}(h)v^{\varepsilon}(k)\bigl[G(y+k,x+h)^{2}-G(y,x+h)^{2}\\
&\hskip 227.62204pt-G(y+k,x)^{2}+G(y,x)^{2}\bigr]\,.\end{split}

We can bound the sum of the first two terms on the right-hand side using the bound (
4.2
) and (
4.4
)

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\int_{|x|,|y|<R}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,G(y,x)^{2}\int\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v^{\varepsilon}(h)v^{\varepsilon}(k)\bigl(G(y+k,x+h)+G(y,x+h)\bigr)\\
&\hskip 199.16928pt\times\big|G(y+k,x+h)-G(y,x+h)\big|\\
&\lesssim\int_{|x|,|y|<R}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,G(y,x)^{2}\int\mathrm{d}h\,\mathrm{d}k\,v^{\varepsilon}(h)v^{\varepsilon}(k)\bigl(G(y+k,x+h)+G(y,x+h)\bigr)\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\times\Bigl[\bigl(\big|\log|y+k-x-h|\big|+\big|\log|y-x-h|\big|\bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{|y-x-h|\leqslant 2|k|}\\
&\hskip 256.0748pt+\frac{|k|}{|y-x-h|}\mathbbm{1}_{|y-x-h|\geqslant 2|k|}\Bigr]\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon R^{C}\,.\end{split}

As for the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.7
)

\begin{split}\|(V-V^{\varepsilon})_{R^{c}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim&\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R-1\\
|\tilde{x}|,|\tilde{y}|>R-1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,\bigl(v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})-\delta(x-\tilde{x})\bigr)\bigl(v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})-\delta(y-\tilde{y})\bigr)\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\times\bigl[G(y,x)^{4}+G(\tilde{y},\tilde{x})^{4}+G(x,\tilde{y})^{4}+G(\tilde{x},y)^{4}\bigr]\\
\lesssim&\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R-1\\
|\tilde{x}|,|\tilde{y}|>R-1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})G(y,x)^{4}\\
\lesssim&\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|,|y|>R-1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,G(y,x)^{4}\lesssim R^{-c}\,,\end{split}

where in the last line we handled the term as the equivalent bound in (
4.6
) in Lemma
4.1
. Hence, we can conclude analogously as Lemma
4.2
.
∎

We can now show the integrability of
\mathrm{e}^{-V^{\varepsilon}}
, uniformly in
\varepsilon
. Analogously as in
(
7
, Sec 4.2)
, the following result holds, which is an adaptation of Nelson’s argument
20
. In the following, we use the notation
(\cdot)^{\pm}
to denote
(\cdot)^{\pm\delta}
for any constant
\delta>0
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\theta>10
. Then there is a constant
c>0
such that for all
\varepsilon>0
,
t\geqslant 1
and
\gamma<\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2\theta}
we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{e}^{-V^{\varepsilon}}>t)\lesssim\exp\Bigl\{-c(\log t)^{(-\frac{1}{2\gamma}+)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\theta})}\Bigr\}.

The same bound holds for
V^{\varepsilon}
replaced by
V
.

The structure of the proof of Proposition
4.3
follows the proof in
(
7
, Prop. 4.3)
. However, there are a few key points that need to be changed in our setting. First, we need to redefine the truncated Green function
G_{N}
and the truncated field
\phi_{N}
with respect to the definition used in
7
. Indeed, since the system is non-translational invariant, we cannot use properties of the Fourier coefficients as on the torus. To this end, we consider the definition of
\phi_{N}
as in (
2.4
), where now we choose the truncation to be
\vartheta(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\mathrm{e}^{-x}
.
Hence, the truncated field (
2.4
) and Green function (
2.6
) are respectively given by

\phi_{N}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{X_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2N}}u_{k}\;,\quad\quad G_{N}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{N}}}{\lambda_{k}}u_{k}u_{k}^{*}\,.

(4.8)

Then, we define the truncated version of
V^{\varepsilon}
, as in (
3.1
), converging to
V^{\varepsilon}
as
N\to\infty

V^{\varepsilon}_{N}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x}){\vcentcolon\!|\phi_{N}(x)|^{2}|\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\,.

(4.9)

Let
(Y_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of i.i.d standard complex Gaussian variables, independent of the family
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. For
0<N\leqslant M\leqslant\infty
we define the field

\psi_{N,M}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{Y_{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\Bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{M}}-\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\lambda_{k}}{N}}\Bigr)^{1/2}u_{k},

by construction
\phi_{N}
and
\psi_{N,M}
are independent. For
M<\infty
, they are almost surely continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
7
7

7

As
U\in L^{\infty}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we can conclude from
(
16
, 10.1 Remark (3))
that the eigenfunctions of (
2.1
) are
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for any
\alpha<1
by elliptic regularity of
h-\lambda
for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
. From (
4.8
) we deduce

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[\psi_{N,M}(x)\overline{\psi}_{N,M}(y)]&=G_{M}(x,y)-G_{N}(x,y)\,.\end{split}

Since
\phi_{N}
and
\psi_{N,M}
are independent, for
N\leqslant M
we can decompose the fields into low and high frequencies
8
8

8

Here
\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}
stands for equality in law.

\phi_{N}+\psi_{N,M}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\phi_{M}

so that for
M=\infty

\phi_{N}+\psi_{N,\infty}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\phi\,.

By the definition of
\phi_{M}
we have, for any
N\leqslant M
,

V_{M}^{\varepsilon}\overset{\mathrm{d}}{=}\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x}){\vcentcolon\!|\phi_{N}(x)+\psi_{N,M}(x)|^{2}|\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})+\psi_{N,M}(\tilde{x})|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}\,.

The following lemma is the first ingredient to show Proposition
4.3
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
s\in(1,2)
satisfy (
2.3
). There exists a constant
C>0
depending on
v
such that almost surely

V_{N}^{\varepsilon}\geqslant-CN^{\gamma}\log N

for all
\varepsilon>0
, and
\gamma=s-1
.

Proof.

Let us denote
S=1+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}x\,|v(x)|
. Using Wick ordered form of (
4.9
) we find the lower bound

\begin{split}V_{N}^{\varepsilon}&=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\Bigl[|\phi_{N}(x)|^{2}|\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})|^{2}-G_{N}(x,x)|\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})|^{2}-G_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x})|\phi_{N}(x)|^{2}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt-2\text{Re}G_{N}(x,\tilde{x})\phi_{N}(x)\overline{\phi}_{N}(\tilde{x})+G_{N}(x,x)G_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x})+G_{N}(x,\tilde{x})^{2}\Bigr]\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\Bigl[\bigl(|\phi_{N}(x)|^{2}-SG_{N}(x,x)\bigr)\bigl(|\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})|^{2}-SG_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x})\bigr)\\
&\hskip 99.58464pt+2(S-1)G_{N}(x,x)|\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})|^{2}-2\text{Re}G_{N}(x,\tilde{x})\phi_{N}(x)\overline{\phi}_{N}(\tilde{x})\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt-(S^{2}-1)G_{N}(x,x)G_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x})+G_{N}(x,\tilde{x})^{2}\Bigr]\\
&\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\Bigl[2(S-1)G_{N}(x,x)|\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})|^{2}-(S^{2}-1)G_{N}(x,x)G_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x})\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt-2\sqrt{G_{N}(x,x)G_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x})}|\phi_{N}(x)||\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})|\Bigr]\\
&\geqslant(S-2)\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})G_{N}(x,x)|\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})|^{2}\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt-\frac{(S^{2}-1)}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})G_{N}(x,x)G_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x})\,.\end{split}

(4.10)

To conclude, on one hand we notice that the first term on the right-hand side is non-negative, by definition of
S
. On the other hand, using that
\mathrm{e}^{-x}\lesssim x^{-\gamma}
for any
x>1
, we have that

\tr(G_{N})=\int\mathrm{d}x\,\int_{1/N}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{e}^{-th}(x,x)=\tr(h^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-h/N})\lesssim N^{\gamma}\tr(h^{-1-\gamma})\,.

Recalling the condition in Remark
2.3
, in order to have
\tr(h^{-1-\gamma})<\infty
we require
\gamma=s-1
. Hence, using that the integral of
v
is one, as well as (
7.2
),
we can bound the second term on the right-hand side of (
4.10
) as

\begin{split}&\frac{(S^{2}-1)}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})G_{N}(x,x)G_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{x})\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\leqslant\frac{(S^{2}-1)}{2}\log N\int\mathrm{d}x\,G_{N}(x,x)\lesssim\frac{(S^{2}-1)}{2}N^{\gamma}\log N\,,\\
\end{split}

which concludes the proof.
∎

The last key lemma to show Proposition
4.3
is an estimate for the
L^{2}
-norm of the difference
V_{N}^{\varepsilon}-V_{M}^{\varepsilon}
, which is the equivalent of
(
7
, Lemma 4.5)
. Also in this case, it is important to use sharper estimates on the decay of the Green function.

Lemma 4.5
.

For any
0<N\leqslant M\leqslant\infty
, and
\theta>2
we have

\|V_{M}^{\varepsilon}-V_{N}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim N^{-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\theta}+}\,.

(4.11)

Remark 4.6
.

The bound in (
4.11
) is essentially optimal. If we compare with the analogous estimate for the homogeneous case from
(
7
, Lemma 4.5)
, the same
L^{2}
-norm was controlled by the stronger bound
N^{-1+}
by regularising the Green function of
\kappa-\Delta/2
with a smooth mollifier. The reason for our weaker bound is twofold. First, we use the exponential regularisation function
\vartheta(x)=\mathrm{e}^{-x}
of the Green function, which is very convenient throughout our analysis, but leads to an optimal bound
N^{-1/2+}
even in the homogeneous case on the torus. Second, if we regularise
G
by convolution with a compactly supported smooth function, as in
7
, we can apply the second-order expansion from
(
7
, Lemma B.3)
, which yields the stronger bound
N^{-1+}
. However, this expansion requires
G
to be locally
C^{2}
, which is in general not true under Assumption
2.1
. Thus, unless we make an additional smoothness assumption on the potential
U
, we believe that Lemma
4.5
cannot be improved.

Assuming a smooth enough potential
U
and regularizing
G
by convolution with a smooth compactly supported function, we believe that our condition
\theta>10
can be improved to
\theta>4
.

Proof.

Following
(
7
, Section 4.2)
, we have that the difference
V_{M}^{\varepsilon}-V_{N}^{\varepsilon}
, for
N\leqslant M
can be rewritten as

V_{M}^{\varepsilon}-V_{N}^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{a,\tilde{a},b,\tilde{b}\in\{0,1\}}\mathbbm{1}_{a+\tilde{a}+b+\tilde{b}>0}V_{N,M}^{\varepsilon}(a,\tilde{a},b,\tilde{b})

(4.12)

where

\begin{split}V_{N,M}^{\varepsilon}(a,\tilde{a},b,\tilde{b})&\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\vcentcolon\phi_{N}(x)^{1-a}\phi_{N}(\tilde{x})^{1-\tilde{a}}\overline{\phi}_{N}(x)^{1-b}\overline{\phi}_{N}(\tilde{x})^{1-\tilde{b}}\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt\times\psi_{N,M}(x)^{a}\psi_{N,M}(\tilde{x})^{\tilde{a}}\overline{\psi}_{N,M}(x)^{b}\overline{\psi}_{N,M}(\tilde{x})^{\tilde{b}}\vcentcolon\,.\\
\end{split}

Hence, by (
4.12
) and Minkowski’s inequality, it suffices to estimate

\mathbb{E}[|V_{N,M}^{\varepsilon}(a,\tilde{a},b,\tilde{b})|^{2}]

for any fixed
a,\tilde{a},b,\tilde{b}\in\{0,1\}
satisfying
a+\tilde{a}+b+\tilde{b}>0
. Using Proposition
7.1
, we have

\begin{split}|G_{M}(x,y)-G_{N}(x,y)|\lesssim q_{N}(x,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\Bigl[\log(N|x-y|^{2})^{-1}\Bigr]_{+}\wedge\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-N|x-y|^{2}}}{N|x-y|^{2}}\,.\end{split}

Then using Wick’s theorem and Young’s inequality we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\bigl[|V_{N,M}^{\varepsilon}(a,\tilde{a},b,\tilde{b})|^{2}\bigr]&=\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})G_{N}(x,y)G_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt\times\Bigl[\bigl(G_{M}(\tilde{y},x)-G_{N}(\tilde{y},x)\bigr)\bigl(G_{M}(y,\tilde{x})-G_{N}(y,\tilde{x})\bigr)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt+\bigl(G_{M}(y,x)-G_{N}(y,x)\bigr)\bigl(G_{M}(\tilde{y},\tilde{x})-G_{N}(\tilde{y},\tilde{x})\bigr)\Bigr]\\
&\lesssim\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})G_{N}(x,y)G_{N}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\\
&\hskip 71.13188pt\times\Bigl[q_{N}(\tilde{y},x)q_{N}(y,\tilde{x})+q_{N}(y,x)q_{N}(\tilde{y},\tilde{x})\Bigr]\\
&\lesssim\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,|v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})||v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})|G_{N}(x,y)^{2}q_{N}(y,x)^{2}\\
&\quad+\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,|v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})||v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})|G_{N}(x,y)^{2}q_{N}(\tilde{y},x)^{2}\\
&\lesssim\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,G_{N}(x,y)^{2}q_{N}(y,x)^{2}\\
&\quad+\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}\,|v^{\varepsilon}(y-\tilde{y})|G_{N}(x,y)^{2}q_{N}(\tilde{y},x)^{2}\,.\end{split}

(4.13)

Using the bound on
q_{N}
and Proposition
7.1
, the first term on the right-hand side of (
4.13
) can be bounded as

\begin{split}&\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,G_{N}(x,y)q_{N}(y,x)\\
&\;\lesssim\int\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\Bigl[\Bigl(\Bigl[\log(|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)\vee\tilde{g}(y)})^{-1}\Bigr]_{+}\wedge\log N\Bigr)\vee 1\Bigr]\mathrm{e}^{-|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)\vee\tilde{g}(y)}}\\
&\hskip 227.62204pt\times\Bigl[\Bigl[\log(N|x-y|^{2})^{-1}\Bigr]_{+}\wedge\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-N|x-y|^{2}}}{N|x-y|^{2}}\Bigr]\\
&\;\lesssim\int_{|x-y|\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\Bigl[\Bigl(\Bigl[-\log(|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)})\Bigr]_{+}\wedge\log N\Bigr)\vee 1\Bigr]\mathrm{e}^{-|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)}}\\
&\hskip 256.0748pt\times\Bigl[-\log(N|x-y|^{2})\Bigr]_{+}\\
&\;+\int_{|x-y|>\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\Bigl[\Bigl(\Bigl[-\log(|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)})\Bigr]_{+}\wedge\log N\Bigr)\vee 1\Bigr]\mathrm{e}^{-|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-N|x-y|^{2}}}{N|x-y|^{2}}\\
&\quad=\rm{(A)}+\rm{(B)}\,.\end{split}

For the term
\rm(A)
we perform a change of variable setting
|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)}=|u|
, and we distinguish the cases when
\tilde{g}(x)/N<1
and
\tilde{g}(x)/N\geqslant 1
. When
\tilde{g}(x)/N<1
we have

\begin{split}\rm{(A)}&=\int_{|u|\leqslant\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{g}(x)}{N}}}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\,\frac{1}{\tilde{g}(x)}\Bigl[\Bigl(\Bigl[-\log(|u|)\Bigr]_{+}\wedge\log N\Bigr)\vee 1\Bigr]\mathrm{e}^{-|u|}\Bigl[-\log(\sqrt{Ng(x)^{-1}}|u|)\Bigr]_{+}\\
&\lesssim\frac{\log N}{N}\int_{|x|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{\theta}}}\mathrm{d}x\lesssim N^{-1+\frac{2}{\theta}+}\,.\end{split}

On the other hand if
\tilde{g}(x)\geqslant N
, we can use the exponential decay and the fact that
\tilde{g}(x)\gtrsim 1+|x|^{\theta}
to have

{\rm(A)}\lesssim\int_{|x|\geqslant N^{\frac{1}{\theta}}}\mathrm{d}x\,\frac{1}{\tilde{g}(x)}(\log N)^{2}\lesssim(\log N)^{2}\int_{N^{\frac{1}{\theta}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}r\,r^{1-\theta}\lesssim N^{\frac{2}{\theta}-1+}\,.

As for the term
\rm(B)
, using
\tilde{g}(x)\geqslant 1
and
|x-y|>1/\sqrt{N}
we have

\begin{split}{\rm(B)}&\lesssim\log N\int_{|x-y|>\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{e}^{-|x-y|\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-N|x-y|^{2}}}{N|x-y|^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\log N\int\mathrm{d}x\,\int_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}r\,r\mathrm{e}^{-r\sqrt{\tilde{g}(x)}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-r^{2}N}}{Nr^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\log N\int_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}r\,r\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-r^{2}N}}{Nr^{2+\frac{4}{\theta}}}\lesssim N^{-1+\frac{2}{\theta}+}\,,\end{split}

where in the third step we used again that
\tilde{g}(x)\gtrsim 1+|x|^{\theta}
.
The second term in the last line on the right-hand side of (
4.13
) can be bounded in a similar way.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

A detailed proof of Proposition
4.3
can be found in
(
7
, Prop. 4.3)
. For completeness we briefly explain the main changes. For any
N\geqslant 1
, and by Lemma
4.4
we have

\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-V^{\varepsilon}}>t\bigr)=\mathbb{P}\bigl(V^{\varepsilon}-V_{N}^{\varepsilon}<-\log t-V^{\varepsilon}_{N}\bigr)\leqslant\mathbb{P}\bigl(V^{\varepsilon}-V_{N}^{\varepsilon}<-(\log t-CN^{\gamma}\log N)\bigr)\,.

Choosing
N\geqslant 1
so that

\log t-CN^{\gamma}\log N=1

for
t
large enough. Using the fact that
V^{\varepsilon}_{M}
is in the 4th polynomial chaos combined with hypercontractivity (see e.g.
(
7
, Appendix A)
) and Lemma
4.5
, we have for any
0<N\leqslant M<\infty
and
p\in 2\mathbb{N}

\|V_{M}^{\varepsilon}-V_{N}^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\frac{p^{2}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\theta}-}}

which holds also for
V^{\varepsilon}_{M}
replaced by
V^{\varepsilon}
by Lemma
4.2
. Hence, by Chebyshev’s inequality, for any
p\in 2\mathbb{N}

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-V^{\varepsilon}}>t\bigr)\leqslant\mathbb{E}\bigl[|V^{\varepsilon}-V^{\varepsilon}_{N}|^{p}]\leqslant\biggl(\frac{Cp^{2}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\theta}-}}\biggr)^{p}&\leqslant\Bigl(Cp^{2}(\log t)^{(-\frac{1}{\gamma}+)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\theta})}\Bigr)^{p}\\
&\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-c(\log t)^{(-\frac{1}{2\gamma}+)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\theta})}}\end{split}

where in the last step we optimised over
p\in 2\mathbb{N}
. For
\gamma<\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2\theta}
and
t
large enough the statement of Proposition
4.3
holds. From (
2.3
) this restriction on
\gamma
implies an upper bound on
s<\frac{5}{4}-\frac{1}{2\theta}
. Together with the lower bound
s>1+\frac{2}{\theta}
in Remark
2.3
we obtain the condition
\theta>10
.
∎

Finally, the integrability of
\mathrm{e}^{-V}
follows from Proposition
4.3
and Lemma
4.2
. To conclude this subsection, we mention that the proof of the first statement in Proposition
3.2
, namely the convergence of the partition functions
\zeta^{W^{\varepsilon}}\to\zeta
, follows from the following result, whose proof can be found in
(
7
, Lemma 4.6)
.

Lemma 4.7
.

For any
1\leqslant p<\infty
we have
\|\mathrm{e}^{-V}-\mathrm{e}^{-W^{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{p}}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

We conclude this subsection, by noting that the results above also imply the integrability of the field
W^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}
(
3.12
). Indeed proceeding similarly as for
W^{\varepsilon}
, we need to introduce a field
V^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}
, analogously as
V^{\varepsilon}
,

V^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}\,\varphi(\eta x)v^{\varepsilon}(x-\tilde{x})\varphi(\eta\tilde{x})\,{\vcentcolon\!|\phi(x)|^{2}|\phi(\tilde{x})|^{2}\!\vcentcolon}

and prove the estimates for the
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
norm of
V^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}-W^{\varepsilon}_{\eta}
. For conciseness, we omit the details here.

4.2.  Correlation functions

In this subsection, we prove the second claim of Proposition
3.2
. The arguments are analogous to those of
(
7
, Section 4.4)
; the main difference is the control of the large scale behaviour of the correlation functions, which we obtain through a representation derived from repeated Gaussian integration by parts. In particular, in our results we can show the local uniform convergence of the Wick-ordered correlation functions with respect to a decaying weight function, which is a stronger result than the desired convergence in
L^{1}\cap L^{\infty}
.

To define the weight function, let
y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
\ell\geqslant 1
and consider the graph of vertices
\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\ell}
that are connected when
\lvert y_{i}-y_{j}\rvert\leqslant 1
. Let
\sim
be the equivalence relation between two vertices
y_{1},y_{2}
such that
y_{1}\sim y_{2}
when
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
belong to the same connected component. For
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\ell}
,
\theta>0
, we define the weight function

\Upsilon_{\theta}(\mathbf{y})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\prod_{x\in\{y_{i}\}_{i}/\sim}\frac{1}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{\theta}}\,,

and for
\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{dp}
the symmetrised weight

\Upsilon_{\theta}(\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\sum_{\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}\in S_{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\Upsilon_{\theta}(x_{i},x_{\pi_{1}(i)},\tilde{x}_{\pi_{2}(i)},\tilde{x}_{\pi_{3}(i)})\,.

It can be checked that, for any
\theta
satisfying the condition in Remark
2.3
, we have
\Upsilon_{\theta}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2dp})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2dp})
.

The claim of (
3.6
) in Proposition
3.2
is now a consequence of the following result.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
d=2
. Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.6
, for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
c>0
, we have

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon\to 0\end{subarray}}\sup_{\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}\frac{|(\widehat{\gamma}_{p})_{\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}-(\widehat{\gamma}_{p}^{W^{\varepsilon}})_{\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}}|}{\Upsilon_{\theta-c}(\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}})}=0\,.

(4.14)

We now prove Proposition
4.8
. To this end, we start by considering the probability space consisting of elements
X=(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
X_{k}\in\mathbb{C}.
We define the first order differential operators

L_{N,x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\sqrt{\vartheta(\delta\lambda_{k})}u_{k}(x)\partial_{\bar{X}_{k}},\quad\bar{L}_{N,x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\hbox{.}}}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\sqrt{\vartheta(\delta\lambda_{k})}\bar{u}_{k}(x)\partial_{X_{k}}.

(4.15)

over the space of random variables
f(X)
, where
f
is smooth in the sense that all its partial derivatives exist. These operators satisfy
L_{N,x}\phi_{N}(y)=0
and
L_{N,x}\bar{\phi}_{N}(y)=G_{N}(x,y)
